Explanation of the Full Working Code

    Installation and Imports:
        Installs easyocr, transformers, torch, torchvision, and matplotlib.
        Imports necessary modules for OCR, image processing, and visualization.

    Model Initialization:
        Initializes EasyOCR for word detection in English.
        Loads the finetuned TrOCR model and its processor.
        Moves the model to GPU if available for faster inference.

    Word Detection:
        Opens the specified full-page image.
        Uses EasyOCR to detect words and obtain their bounding boxes.

    Sorting Bounding Boxes:
        Sorts the detected bounding boxes in reading order (top-to-bottom, left-to-right) to maintain the correct sequence of words.

    Text Recognition:
        Iterates over each sorted bounding box.
        Extracts the word image with a small padding to ensure clarity.
        Uses TrOCR to recognize the text from each word image.
        Collects all recognized texts in order.

    Assembling Text:
        Joins all recognized words into a single string, separated by spaces.
        Saves the final recognized text to a .txt file.

    Visualization (Optional):
        Draws bounding boxes and overlays the recognized text on the original image.
        Displays the annotated image using Matplotlib for verification.

    Saving and Downloading Text:
        Saves the recognized text to a file and provides an option to download it directly from Colab.

Additional Considerations
1. Preserving Line Structure

The above approach assembles words in reading order but doesn't preserve the original line breaks. To maintain the structure, you can group words into lines based on their y-coordinates. Here's an enhanced approach:

from collections import defaultdict

def group_words_into_lines(results, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, threshold=10):
    """
    Groups words into lines based on their y-coordinates.
    """
    lines = defaultdict(list)
    
    # Iterate over sorted bounding boxes and texts
    for bbox, text in zip(sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
        # Calculate the average y-coordinate of the bounding box
        avg_y = np.mean([point[1] for point in bbox])
        
        # Find the line that this word belongs to
        assigned = False
        for line_y in lines:
            if abs(avg_y - line_y) < threshold:
                lines[line_y].append((bbox, text))
                assigned = True
                break
        if not assigned:
            lines[avg_y].append((bbox, text))
    
    # Sort lines top to bottom
    sorted_lines = sorted(lines.items(), key=lambda x: x[0])
    
    # Within each line, sort words left to right
    final_lines = []
    for _, words in sorted_lines:
        sorted_words = sorted(words, key=lambda x: x[0][0][0])  # Sort by x1
        line_text = ' '.join([word[1] for word in sorted_words])
        final_lines.append(line_text)
    
    return final_lines

# Group words into lines
lines = group_words_into_lines(results, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, threshold=15)

# Combine lines with newline characters
final_text_with_lines = '\n'.join(lines)

print("Recognized Text with Lines:\n")
print(final_text_with_lines)

# Save to a separate file
output_text_lines_path = "/content/recognized_journal_page_with_lines.txt"

with open(output_text_lines_path, "w") as f:
    f.write(final_text_with_lines)

print(f"Recognized text with lines saved to {output_text_lines_path}")

# Optionally, download the file
files.download(output_text_lines_path)

    Explanation:

        Grouping Threshold: Adjust the threshold parameter based on the spacing between lines in your journal page.
        Line Sorting: Groups words into lines by comparing their average y-coordinates.
        Word Sorting within Lines: Ensures words within a line are ordered from left to right.

2. Handling Multiple Columns

If your journal page has multiple columns, additional logic is required to group lines into columns based on x-coordinates. This involves clustering bounding boxes horizontally and then applying the line grouping within each column.
3. Error Handling

Implement error handling to manage cases where word extraction or recognition fails.

def recognize_text_safe(word_image, processor, model, device):
    """
    Safely recognizes text from a word image using TrOCR.
    Returns empty string if recognition fails.
    """
    try:
        return recognize_text(word_image, processor, model, device)
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error recognizing text: {e}")
        return ""

4. Performance Optimization

    Batch Processing: Modify the recognition function to process multiple word images in batches for improved performance.
    Parallel Processing: Utilize multiprocessing or parallelism to speed up the extraction and recognition steps, especially for large pages.

5. Quality of Input Images

Ensure that the input image is clear and properly preprocessed (e.g., thresholded, denoised) to improve OCR accuracy.
Conclusion

By integrating EasyOCR for precise word detection and TrOCR for accurate text recognition, you can effectively digitize handwritten journal pages, preserving both the content and the structure. The provided code example offers a robust starting point, which you can further customize based on the specific layout and requirements of your journal pages.

Feel free to reach out if you encounter any issues or need further assistance!
You said:
An error running this code: AttributeError: 'ImageDraw' object has no attribute 'textsize'

Troubleshooting code integration

I encountered an AttributeError with ImageDraw.textsize while integrating EasyOCR with TrOCR. I suspect changes in Pillow's version may have replaced or updated the method.

Identifying alternatives

I found that replacing ImageDraw.textsize with ImageFont.getsize in the code might resolve the AttributeError, considering changes in newer Pillow versions.

Revising visualization methods

I should provide an updated visualize_predictions function that replaces draw.textsize with font.getsize to correct the AttributeError and ensure compatibility with newer Pillow versions.

Apologies for the inconvenience caused by the error you're encountering. The error message:

AttributeError: 'ImageDraw' object has no attribute 'textsize'

indicates that the ImageDraw object from the Pillow library does not have a textsize method in the version you're using. This discrepancy arises because Pillow has evolved over time, and certain methods have been deprecated or replaced in newer versions.

To resolve this issue, we'll update the visualize_predictions function to use the appropriate method for calculating text size based on your Pillow version. Additionally, I'll provide the complete revised code to ensure seamless integration of EasyOCR and TrOCR for your OCR task.
1. Understanding the Issue
1.1. Cause of the Error

    ImageDraw.textsize Deprecated: In newer versions of Pillow (from 8.0.0 onwards), the textsize method has been deprecated in favor of textbbox or methods from the ImageFont module.

    Solution: Use ImageFont.getsize or ImageFont.getbbox to calculate the size of the text instead of ImageDraw.textsize.

1.2. Verifying Pillow Version

First, let's check the version of Pillow installed in your environment to understand which methods are available.

import PIL
print(PIL.__version__)

Recommendation:

    If Pillow >= 8.0.0: Use ImageFont.getsize or ImageFont.getbbox.
    If Pillow < 8.0.0: ImageDraw.textsize should be available, but it's advisable to upgrade to the latest version for better functionality and support.

2. Updating the visualize_predictions Function

We'll modify the visualize_predictions function to use ImageFont.getsize for calculating text size, ensuring compatibility across Pillow versions.
2.1. Revised visualize_predictions Function

def visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
    """
    Draws bounding boxes and recognized text on the image.
    """
    # Convert image to RGB if not already
    image = image.convert("RGB")
    draw = ImageDraw.Draw(image)

    # Specify a font. You can change the path to a .ttf file if desired.
    try:
        # Attempt to use a TrueType font
        font = ImageFont.truetype("arial.ttf", 15)
    except IOError:
        # Fallback to the default PIL font if the specified font is not found
        font = ImageFont.load_default()

    # Iterate over bounding boxes and recognized texts
    for bbox, text in zip(sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
        # Draw bounding box
        draw.line([tuple(bbox[0]), tuple(bbox[1]), tuple(bbox[2]), tuple(bbox[3]), tuple(bbox[0])], fill="red", width=2)
        
        # Calculate position for text
        x, y = bbox[0]
        
        # Calculate text size using ImageFont
        text_width, text_height = font.getsize(text)
        
        # Draw text background for better visibility
        draw.rectangle([x, y - text_height, x + text_width, y], fill="yellow")
        
        # Draw text
        draw.text((x, y - text_height), text, fill="black", font=font)
    
    # Display the image
    plt.figure(figsize=(15, 15))
    plt.imshow(image)
    plt.axis('off')
    plt.show()

2.2. Explanation of Changes

    Using ImageFont.getsize: This method calculates the width and height of the given text, which we use to create a background rectangle for better text visibility.

    Font Handling:
        Attempt to Load a TrueType Font: Tries to load arial.ttf. If not found, falls back to the default PIL font.
        Custom Fonts: If you have a specific font you'd like to use, provide the path to the .ttf file.

    Drawing Enhancements:
        Background Rectangle: Adds a yellow rectangle behind the text to enhance readability against varied backgrounds.
        Consistent Text Positioning: Ensures that the text is placed just above the bounding box.

3. Complete Revised Code Example

    Recognizes text from a word image using TrOCR.
    """
    # Preprocess the image
    pixel_values = processor(images=word_image, return_tensors="pt").pixel_values
    pixel_values = pixel_values.to(device)
    
    # Generate token IDs
    with torch.no_grad():
        generated_ids = model.generate(pixel_values)
    
    # Decode the token IDs to get the recognized text
    generated_text = processor.batch_decode(generated_ids, skip_special_tokens=True)[0]
    
    return generated_text

# Initialize list to hold recognized texts
recognized_texts = []

# Iterate over sorted bounding boxes
for bbox in sorted_bboxes:
    # Extract word image
    word_img = extract_word_image(image, bbox, padding=5)
    
    # Recognize text using TrOCR
    text = recognize_text(word_img, processor, model, device)
    
    recognized_texts.append(text)

# Combine recognized texts into a single string
final_text = ' '.join(recognized_texts)

print("Recognized Text:\n")
print(final_text)

# Step 7: Assemble Recognized Text into a Document
# Save the final text to a .txt file
output_text_path = "/content/recognized_journal_page.txt"

with open(output_text_path, "w") as f:
    f.write(final_text)

print(f"Recognized text saved to {output_text_path}")

# Step 8: (Optional) Visualize Bounding Boxes and Recognized Text
def visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
    """
    Draws bounding boxes and recognized text on the image.
    """
    # Convert image to RGB if not already
    image = image.convert("RGB")
    draw = ImageDraw.Draw(image)

    # Specify a font. You can change the path to a .ttf file if desired.
    try:
        # Attempt to use a TrueType font
        font = ImageFont.truetype("arial.ttf", 15)
    except IOError:
        # Fallback to the default PIL font if the specified font is not found
        font = ImageFont.load_default()

    # Iterate over bounding boxes and recognized texts
    for bbox, text in zip(sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
        # Draw bounding box
        draw.line([tuple(bbox[0]), tuple(bbox[1]), tuple(bbox[2]), tuple(bbox[3]), tuple(bbox[0])], fill="red", width=2)
        
        # Calculate position for text
        x, y = bbox[0]
        
        # Calculate text size using ImageFont
        text_width, text_height = font.getsize(text)
        
        # Draw text background for better visibility
        draw.rectangle([x, y - text_height, x + text_width, y], fill="yellow")
        
        # Draw text
        draw.text((x, y - text_height), text, fill="black", font=font)
    
    # Display the image
    plt.figure(figsize=(15, 15))
    plt.imshow(image)
    plt.axis('off')
    plt.show()

# Visualize the predictions
visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts)

# Step 9: Save Recognized Text to a File (Already Done in Step 7)
# To download the file from Colab
from google.colab import files
files.download(output_text_path)

4. Additional Enhancements
4.1. Preserving Line Structure

If your journal pages contain multiple lines and you wish to preserve the original line breaks, you can group words into lines based on their y-coordinates. Here's how to enhance the grouping mechanism:

from collections import defaultdict

def group_words_into_lines(results, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, threshold=10):
    """
    Groups words into lines based on their y-coordinates.
    """
    lines = defaultdict(list)
    
    # Iterate over sorted bounding boxes and texts
    for bbox, text in zip(sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
        # Calculate the average y-coordinate of the bounding box
        avg_y = np.mean([point[1] for point in bbox])
        
        # Find the line that this word belongs to
        assigned = False
        for line_y in lines:
            if abs(avg_y - line_y) < threshold:
                lines[line_y].append((bbox, text))
                assigned = True
                break
        if not assigned:
            lines[avg_y].append((bbox, text))
    
    # Sort lines top to bottom
    sorted_lines = sorted(lines.items(), key=lambda x: x[0])
    
    # Within each line, sort words left to right
    final_lines = []
    for _, words in sorted_lines:
        sorted_words = sorted(words, key=lambda x: x[0][0][0])  # Sort by x1
        line_text = ' '.join([word[1] for word in sorted_words])
        final_lines.append(line_text)
    
    return final_lines

# Group words into lines
lines = group_words_into_lines(results, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, threshold=15)

# Combine lines with newline characters
final_text_with_lines = '\n'.join(lines)

print("Recognized Text with Lines:\n")
print(final_text_with_lines)

# Save to a separate file
output_text_lines_path = "/content/recognized_journal_page_with_lines.txt"

with open(output_text_lines_path, "w") as f:
    f.write(final_text_with_lines)

print(f"Recognized text with lines saved to {output_text_lines_path}")

# Optionally, download the file
files.download(output_text_lines_path)

Explanation:

    Grouping Threshold: The threshold parameter determines how close the y-coordinates of words need to be to be considered part of the same line. Adjust this based on the spacing in your journal pages.

    Line Sorting: Ensures that lines are ordered from top to bottom.

    Word Sorting within Lines: Maintains the left-to-right order of words within each line.

4.2. Handling Multiple Columns

If your journal pages have multiple columns, you can extend the grouping logic to cluster words horizontally before grouping them into lines.
4.3. Error Handling

Implement error handling to manage cases where word extraction or recognition might fail.

def recognize_text_safe(word_image, processor, model, device):
    """
    Safely recognizes text from a word image using TrOCR.
    Returns an empty string if recognition fails.
    """
    try:
        return recognize_text(word_image, processor, model, device)
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error recognizing text: {e}")
        return ""

Usage:

Replace the recognition line in the loop with the safe function.

# Recognize text using TrOCR safely
text = recognize_text_safe(word_img, processor, model, device)

4.4. Optimizing Inference Speed

For large images or numerous words, consider batching the recognition process to leverage GPU parallelism.

def batch_recognize_text(word_images, processor, model, device, batch_size=32):
    """
    Recognizes text from a list of word images using TrOCR in batches.
    """
    recognized_texts = []
    for i in range(0, len(word_images), batch_size):
        batch_images = word_images[i:i+batch_size]
        
        # Preprocess images
        pixel_values = processor(images=batch_images, return_tensors="pt").pixel_values
        pixel_values = pixel_values.to(device)
        
        # Generate token IDs
        with torch.no_grad():
            generated_ids = model.generate(pixel_values)
        
        # Decode token IDs to texts
        texts = processor.batch_decode(generated_ids, skip_special_tokens=True)
        recognized_texts.extend(texts)
    
    return recognized_texts

# Collect all word images
word_images = [extract_word_image(image, bbox, padding=5) for bbox in sorted_bboxes]

# Perform batch OCR
recognized_texts = batch_recognize_text(word_images, processor, model, device, batch_size=32)

Explanation:

    Batch Processing: Processes multiple word images simultaneously, which can significantly speed up inference on GPUs.

    Adjustable batch_size: Modify the batch_size parameter based on your GPU's memory capacity.

5. Final Notes

    Font Availability: Ensure that the font specified in ImageFont.truetype (e.g., "arial.ttf") is available in your Colab environment. If not, either upload the font file or use a different one. Alternatively, rely on the default font as a fallback.

    Directory Paths: Confirm that the model_path and image_path correctly point to your finetuned model directory and the input image, respectively.

    Downloading Files: The files.download function allows you to download the recognized text files directly from Colab. Ensure that the files are correctly saved before attempting to download.

    Visualization: The visualize_predictions function provides a visual verification of OCR accuracy. Review the annotated image to identify and rectify any discrepancies.

    Performance: For very large images or high-resolution scans, consider downscaling the images to balance between OCR accuracy and processing speed.

By implementing the above corrections and enhancements, your integration of EasyOCR and TrOCR should function smoothly, allowing you to accurately detect and recognize handwritten words on your journal pages. If you encounter any further issues or have additional questions, feel free to ask!

In google colab enterprise, is there a way to split a code cell?

Confirming features

I'm thinking through whether Google Colab Enterprise supports splitting code cells with keyboard shortcuts, as the free version does.

Confirming split functionality

Yes, in Google Colab Enterprise, you can split code cells using keyboard shortcuts or the menu. This functionality is consistent across different Colab versions.

Yes, in Google Colab Enterprise, you can split a code cell into multiple cells. This feature enhances code organization, readability, and maintainability, especially when working on large projects or collaborative environments. Below is a comprehensive guide on how to achieve this, along with additional tips to optimize your workflow.
1. Understanding Cell Splitting in Google Colab

Cell splitting allows you to divide a single code cell into two separate cells at a specified cursor position. This is particularly useful when you want to:

    Separate logically distinct sections of code.
    Improve readability by breaking down complex code blocks.
    Collaborate more effectively by isolating specific code segments.

2. Methods to Split a Code Cell

There are two primary ways to split a code cell in Google Colab Enterprise:

    Using Keyboard Shortcuts
    Using the Menu Options

2.1. Splitting Using Keyboard Shortcuts

Keyboard shortcuts provide a quick and efficient way to split cells without navigating through menus.
Steps:

    Select the Cell:
        Click on the code cell you wish to split to ensure it's active.

    Position the Cursor:
        Click within the cell to place the cursor at the exact point where you want to split the cell.

    Execute the Split:
        For Windows/Linux:
            Press Ctrl + Shift + - (Control + Shift + Minus)
        For macOS:
            Press Cmd + Shift + - (Command + Shift + Minus)

Example:

Suppose you have the following code in a single cell:

import numpy as np

def add(a, b):
    return a + b

result = add(5, 3)
print(result)

If you place the cursor after the def add(a, b): line and execute the split shortcut, the cell will be divided into two:

Cell 1:

import numpy as np

def add(a, b):

Cell 2:

    return a + b

result = add(5, 3)
print(result)

2.2. Splitting Using Menu Options

If you prefer using the mouse or navigating through menus, Google Colab provides options to split cells via the toolbar.
Steps:

    Select the Cell:
        Click on the code cell you want to split to make it active.

    Navigate to the Edit Menu:
        At the top of the Colab interface, click on Edit.

    Choose Split Cell:
        From the dropdown, select Split cell.

    (Note: Replace with actual image if possible)

    Position the Cursor:
        A dialog may prompt you to confirm the split point. If not, ensure your cursor is at the desired split location within the cell before selecting the option.

3. Additional Tips for Effective Cell Management
3.1. Merging Cells

Just as you can split cells, you can also merge multiple cells into one. This is useful when you want to consolidate related code segments.

    Keyboard Shortcut:
        Select the cells you want to merge.
        Press Ctrl + M + M (Windows/Linux) or Cmd + M + M (macOS).

    Menu Option:
        Select the cells.
        Go to Edit > Merge cells.

3.2. Using Markdown Cells for Documentation

Enhance your notebook's readability by using Markdown cells to add explanations, headings, and comments.

    Inserting a Markdown Cell:
        Click the + Text button above or below existing cells.
        Alternatively, press Esc to enter Command mode, then press M to convert a selected cell to Markdown.

3.3. Keyboard Shortcuts Overview

Familiarizing yourself with common keyboard shortcuts can significantly speed up your workflow.
Action	Shortcut (Windows/Linux)	Shortcut (macOS)
Insert Cell Above	Ctrl + M + A	Cmd + M + A
Insert Cell Below	Ctrl + M + B	Cmd + M + B
Delete Cell	Ctrl + M + D	Cmd + M + D
Move Cell Up	Ctrl + M + K	Cmd + M + K
Move Cell Down	Ctrl + M + J	Cmd + M + J
Toggle Code/Markdown	Ctrl + M + M	Cmd + M + M
Run Selected Cell	Shift + Enter	Shift + Enter

(Note: The exact shortcuts might vary slightly based on updates or custom configurations.)
4. Troubleshooting Common Issues
4.1. Keyboard Shortcuts Not Working

    Check Keyboard Settings:
        Ensure that your keyboard layout is correctly set, especially if using non-standard layouts.

    Browser Conflicts:
        Sometimes, browser extensions or settings might interfere with shortcuts. Try disabling extensions or using a different browser.

    Focus on the Correct Cell:
        Ensure that the cell you want to split is active and that the cursor is positioned correctly within the cell.

4.2. Menu Option Not Visible

    Interface Layout:
        If the Edit menu isn't visible, ensure that you're in the correct mode (not in presentation or another restricted mode).

    Refresh the Page:
        Sometimes, a simple refresh can resolve UI glitches.

5. Complete Example Workflow

To demonstrate cell splitting, let's walk through an example in Google Colab Enterprise.
5.1. Initial Single Cell

# Initial single cell with multiple lines of code
import numpy as np

def multiply(a, b):
    return a * b

result = multiply(4, 5)
print("Result:", result)

5.2. Splitting the Cell

    Position the Cursor:
        Click after the import numpy as np line.

    Execute Split:
        Press Ctrl + Shift + - (Windows/Linux) or Cmd + Shift + - (macOS).

5.3. Resulting Cells

Cell 1:

import numpy as np

Cell 2:

def multiply(a, b):
    return a * b

result = multiply(4, 5)
print("Result:", result)

6. Best Practices for Cell Management

    Logical Segmentation:
        Split cells based on functionality. For instance, import statements in one cell, function definitions in another, and execution code in subsequent cells.

    Documentation:
        Use Markdown cells to describe what each code cell does. This is invaluable for collaboration and future reference.

    Avoid Overly Long Cells:
        Extremely long cells can be hard to navigate and debug. Splitting them improves readability and maintainability.

    Consistent Formatting:
        Maintain consistent indentation and coding standards across cells to ensure clarity.

7. Conclusion

Splitting cells in Google Colab Enterprise is straightforward and enhances your ability to manage and organize your code effectively. Whether you prefer using keyboard shortcuts or menu options, both methods are readily available to suit your workflow preferences. By implementing these practices, you can create more readable, maintainable, and collaborative notebooks.

If you encounter any further issues or have additional questions about Google Colab features, feel free to ask!

Apologies for the inconvenience caused by the error you're encountering. The error message:

AttributeError: 'FreeTypeFont' object has no attribute 'getsize'

indicates that the FreeTypeFont object from the Pillow library does not have a getsize method in the version you're using. This discrepancy arises because Pillow has evolved over time, and certain methods have been deprecated or replaced in newer versions.

To resolve this issue, we'll update the visualize_predictions function to use the appropriate method for calculating text size based on your Pillow version. Additionally, I'll provide the complete revised code to ensure seamless integration of EasyOCR and TrOCR for your OCR task.
1. Understanding the Issue
1.1. Cause of the Error

    ImageDraw.textsize Deprecated: In newer versions of Pillow (from 8.0.0 onwards), the textsize method has been deprecated in favor of textbbox or methods from the ImageFont module.

    Solution: Use ImageFont.getbbox to calculate the size of the text instead of ImageDraw.textsize.

1.2. Verifying Pillow Version

First, let's check the version of Pillow installed in your environment to understand which methods are available.

import PIL
print(PIL.__version__)

Recommendation:

    If Pillow >= 8.0.0: Use ImageFont.getbbox.
    If Pillow < 8.0.0: Consider upgrading to the latest version for better functionality and support.

Upgrade Pillow (if necessary):

!pip install --upgrade Pillow

2. Updating the visualize_predictions Function

We'll modify the visualize_predictions function to use ImageFont.getbbox for calculating text size, ensuring compatibility across Pillow versions.
2.1. Revised visualize_predictions Function

def visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
    """
    Draws bounding boxes and recognized text on the image.
    """
    from PIL import ImageDraw, ImageFont
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

    # Convert image to RGB if not already
    image = image.convert("RGB")
    draw = ImageDraw.Draw(image)

    # Specify a font. You can change the path to a .ttf file if desired.
    try:
        # Attempt to use a TrueType font
        font = ImageFont.truetype("arial.ttf", 15)
    except IOError:
        # Fallback to the default PIL font if the specified font is not found
        font = ImageFont.load_default()

    # Iterate over bounding boxes and recognized texts
    for bbox, text in zip(sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
        # Draw bounding box
        draw.line(
            [tuple(bbox[0]), tuple(bbox[1]), tuple(bbox[2]), tuple(bbox[3]), tuple(bbox[0])],
            fill="red",
            width=2
        )

        # Calculate position for text
        x, y = bbox[0]

        # Calculate text size using ImageFont.getbbox
        text_bbox = font.getbbox(text)
        text_width = text_bbox[2] - text_bbox[0]
        text_height = text_bbox[3] - text_bbox[1]

        # Draw text background for better visibility
        draw.rectangle([x, y - text_height, x + text_width, y], fill="yellow")

        # Draw text
        draw.text((x, y - text_height), text, fill="black", font=font)

    # Display the image
    plt.figure(figsize=(15, 15))
    plt.imshow(image)
    plt.axis('off')
    plt.show()

2.2. Explanation of Changes

    Using ImageFont.getbbox: This method calculates the bounding box of the given text, which we use to determine the width and height for the background rectangle.

    Font Handling:
        Attempt to Load a TrueType Font: Tries to load arial.ttf. If not found, falls back to the default PIL font.
        Custom Fonts: If you have a specific font you'd like to use, provide the path to the .ttf file.

    Drawing Enhancements:
        Background Rectangle: Adds a yellow rectangle behind the text to enhance readability against varied backgrounds.
        Consistent Text Positioning: Ensures that the text is placed just above the bounding box.

3. Complete Revised Code Example

Below is the entire revised workflow incorporating the updated visualize_predictions function. This code integrates EasyOCR for word detection and TrOCR for text recognition, assembles the recognized text into a document, and visualizes the results with bounding boxes and recognized text.

print(f"Detected {len(results)} words.")

# Step 5: Sort Bounding Boxes in Reading Order
def sort_bounding_boxes(results):
    """
    Sort bounding boxes from top to bottom and left to right.
    """
    # Extract bounding boxes and associated data
    data = []
    for result in results:
        bbox = result[0]  # Bounding box
        text = result[1]  # Detected text (unused)
        conf = result[2]  # Confidence (optional)
        # Calculate the average y-coordinate for top of the box
        avg_y = np.mean([point[1] for point in bbox])
        data.append((bbox, avg_y))
    
    # Sort primarily by avg_y (top to bottom), then by x-coordinate (left to right)
    sorted_data = sorted(data, key=lambda x: (x[1], x[0][0][0]))
    sorted_bboxes = [item[0] for item in sorted_data]
    return sorted_bboxes

sorted_bboxes = sort_bounding_boxes(results)

# Step 6: Extract Word Images and Recognize Text with TrOCR
def extract_word_image(image, bbox, padding=5):
    """
    Extracts a word image from the full-page image using the bounding box.
    Adds padding to include surrounding pixels.
    """
    # Unpack bounding box coordinates
    x1, y1 = bbox[0]
    x2, y2 = bbox[2]
    
    # Add padding
    x1 = max(int(x1) - padding, 0)
    y1 = max(int(y1) - padding, 0)
    x2 = min(int(x2) + padding, image.width)
    y2 = min(int(y2) + padding, image.height)
    
    # Crop the image
    cropped_image = image.crop((x1, y1, x2, y2))
    
    return cropped_image

def recognize_text(word_image, processor, model, device):

    pixel_values = pixel_values.to(device)
    
    # Generate token IDs

        generated_ids = model.generate(pixel_values)
    
    # Decode the token IDs to get the recognized text
    generated_text = processor.batch_decode(generated_ids, skip_special_tokens=True)[0]
    
    return generated_text

# Initialize list to hold recognized texts

    # Recognize text using TrOCR

    recognized_texts.append(text)

# Combine recognized texts into a single string
final_text = ' '.join(recognized_texts)

print("Recognized Text:\n")
print(final_text)

# Step 7: Assemble Recognized Text into a Document
# Save the final text to a .txt file
output_text_path = "/content/recognized_journal_page.txt"

with open(output_text_path, "w") as f:

print(f"Recognized text saved to {output_text_path}")

4. Additional Enhancements
4.1. Preserving Line Structure

If your journal pages contain multiple lines and you wish to preserve the original line breaks, you can group words into lines based on their y-coordinates. Here's how to enhance the grouping mechanism:

from collections import defaultdict

def group_words_into_lines(results, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, threshold=10):
    """
    Groups words into lines based on their y-coordinates.
    """
    lines = defaultdict(list)
    
    # Iterate over sorted bounding boxes and texts

        for line_y in lines:
            if abs(avg_y - line_y) < threshold:
                lines[line_y].append((bbox, text))
                assigned = True
                break
        if not assigned:
            lines[avg_y].append((bbox, text))
    
    # Sort lines top to bottom
    sorted_lines = sorted(lines.items(), key=lambda x: x[0])
    
    # Within each line, sort words left to right
    final_lines = []
    for _, words in sorted_lines:
        sorted_words = sorted(words, key=lambda x: x[0][0][0])  # Sort by x1
        line_text = ' '.join([word[1] for word in sorted_words])
        final_lines.append(line_text)
    
    return final_lines

# Group words into lines
lines = group_words_into_lines(results, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, threshold=15)

# Combine lines with newline characters
final_text_with_lines = '\n'.join(lines)

print("Recognized Text with Lines:\n")
print(final_text_with_lines)

# Save to a separate file
output_text_lines_path = "/content/recognized_journal_page_with_lines.txt"

with open(output_text_lines_path, "w") as f:
    f.write(final_text_with_lines)

print(f"Recognized text with lines saved to {output_text_lines_path}")

# Optionally, download the file
files.download(output_text_lines_path)

Explanation:

    Grouping Threshold: The threshold parameter determines how close the y-coordinates of words need to be to be considered part of the same line. Adjust this based on the spacing in your journal pages.

    Line Sorting: Ensures that lines are ordered from top to bottom.

    Word Sorting within Lines: Maintains the left-to-right order of words within each line.

4.2. Handling Multiple Columns

If your journal pages have multiple columns, you can extend the grouping logic to cluster words horizontally before grouping them into lines. This involves identifying clusters of words based on their x-coordinates and then applying the line grouping within each cluster.

Basic Approach:

    Cluster Words into Columns:
        Analyze the x-coordinates of bounding boxes to identify distinct columns.
    Group Words into Lines within Each Column:
        Apply the line grouping mechanism separately within each column.

Note: Implementing a robust multi-column detection mechanism can be complex and may require additional libraries or more sophisticated algorithms (e.g., using clustering techniques like K-Means).
4.3. Error Handling

Implement error handling to manage cases where word extraction or recognition might fail.

def recognize_text_safe(word_image, processor, model, device):
    """
    Safely recognizes text from a word image using TrOCR.
    Returns an empty string if recognition fails.
    """
    try:
        return recognize_text(word_image, processor, model, device)
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error recognizing text: {e}")
        return ""

Usage:

Replace the recognition line in the loop with the safe function.

# Recognize text using TrOCR safely
text = recognize_text_safe(word_img, processor, model, device)

4.4. Optimizing Inference Speed

For large images or numerous words, consider batching the recognition process to leverage GPU parallelism.

def batch_recognize_text(word_images, processor, model, device, batch_size=32):
    """
    Recognizes text from a list of word images using TrOCR in batches.
    """
    recognized_texts = []
    for i in range(0, len(word_images), batch_size):
        batch_images = word_images[i:i+batch_size]
        
        # Preprocess images
        pixel_values = processor(images=batch_images, return_tensors="pt").pixel_values
        pixel_values = pixel_values.to(device)
        
        # Generate token IDs
        with torch.no_grad():
            generated_ids = model.generate(pixel_values)
        
        # Decode token IDs to texts
        texts = processor.batch_decode(generated_ids, skip_special_tokens=True)
        recognized_texts.extend(texts)
    
    return recognized_texts

# Collect all word images
word_images = [extract_word_image(image, bbox, padding=5) for bbox in sorted_bboxes]

# Perform batch OCR
recognized_texts = batch_recognize_text(word_images, processor, model, device, batch_size=32)

Explanation:

    Batch Processing: Processes multiple word images simultaneously, which can significantly speed up inference on GPUs.

    Adjustable batch_size: Modify the batch_size parameter based on your GPU's memory capacity.

5. Final Notes

    Font Availability: Ensure that the font specified in ImageFont.truetype (e.g., "arial.ttf") is available in your Colab environment. If not, either upload the font file or use a different one. Alternatively, rely on the default font as a fallback.

    Directory Paths: Confirm that the model_path and image_path correctly point to your finetuned model directory and the input image, respectively.

    Downloading Files: The files.download function allows you to download the recognized text files directly from Colab. Ensure that the files are correctly saved before attempting to download.

    Visualization: The visualize_predictions function provides a visual verification of OCR accuracy. Review the annotated image to identify and rectify any discrepancies.

    Performance: For very large images or high-resolution scans, consider downscaling the images to balance between OCR accuracy and processing speed.

6. Conclusion

By implementing the above corrections and enhancements, your integration of EasyOCR and TrOCR should function smoothly, allowing you to accurately detect and recognize handwritten words on your journal pages. Here's a quick recap:

    Set Up Environment: Install and import necessary libraries.
    Load Models: Initialize EasyOCR and load your finetuned TrOCR model.
    Detect Words: Use EasyOCR to obtain bounding boxes for words.
    Sort and Group Words: Arrange words in reading order and optionally group them into lines.
    Recognize Text: Extract word images and use TrOCR for text recognition.
    Assemble Text: Combine recognized words into a coherent document.
    Visualize Results: Optionally, draw bounding boxes and overlay recognized text for verification.
    Save and Download: Save the recognized text to files and download them as needed.

To enhance the visibility of the recognized text on your image, you can increase the font size and adjust the display size of the image. 1. Understanding the Components Affecting Text Size

There are two main factors influencing the perceived size of the text in the visualization:

    Font Size in ImageFont.truetype: Determines the actual size of the text being drawn on the image.
    Figure Size in Matplotlib (plt.figure(figsize=(width, height))): Controls the display size of the image within the notebook. A larger figure size can make the text appear bigger when rendered.

2. Modifying the visualize_predictions Function

We'll adjust both the font size and the figure size to improve text visibility.
2.1. Revised visualize_predictions Function

Here's the updated function with increased font size and figure size:

def visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, font_size=20, padding=10):
    """
    Draws bounding boxes and recognized text on the image.

    Parameters:
    - image (PIL.Image): The original image.
    - sorted_bboxes (list): List of bounding boxes sorted in reading order.
    - recognized_texts (list): List of recognized texts corresponding to the bounding boxes.
    - font_size (int): Size of the font for the recognized text.
    - padding (int): Padding around the text background for better visibility.
    """
    from PIL import ImageDraw, ImageFont
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

    # Convert image to RGB if not already
    image = image.convert("RGB")
    draw = ImageDraw.Draw(image)

    # Specify a font. You can change the path to a .ttf file if desired.
    try:
        # Attempt to use a TrueType font with the desired font size
        font = ImageFont.truetype("arial.ttf", font_size)
    except IOError:
        # Fallback to the default PIL font if the specified font is not found
        print("Specified font not found. Using default font.")
        font = ImageFont.load_default()

    # Iterate over bounding boxes and recognized texts
    for bbox, text in zip(sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
        # Draw bounding box
        draw.line(
            [tuple(bbox[0]), tuple(bbox[1]), tuple(bbox[2]), tuple(bbox[3]), tuple(bbox[0])],
            fill="red",
            width=2
        )

        # Calculate position for text (top-left corner of the bounding box)
        x, y = bbox[0]

        # Calculate text size using ImageFont.getbbox (returns (left, top, right, bottom))
        text_bbox = font.getbbox(text)
        text_width = text_bbox[2] - text_bbox[0]
        text_height = text_bbox[3] - text_bbox[1]

        # Adjust y position to place text above the bounding box
        y_text = y - text_height - padding if y - text_height - padding > 0 else y + padding

        # Draw text background rectangle
        draw.rectangle([x, y_text, x + text_width, y_text + text_height], fill="yellow")

        # Draw text
        draw.text((x, y_text), text, fill="black", font=font)

    # Display the image with increased figure size for better visibility
    plt.figure(figsize=(20, 20))  # Increased from (15, 15) to (20, 20)
    plt.imshow(image)
    plt.axis('off')
    plt.show()

2.2. Explanation of Changes

    Font Size Adjustment:
        Parameter font_size=20: Introduced a parameter to easily adjust the font size. You can modify this value based on your preference.
        ImageFont.truetype("arial.ttf", font_size): Sets the font size to the specified font_size. If "arial.ttf" is unavailable, it falls back to the default font.

    Figure Size Adjustment:
        plt.figure(figsize=(20, 20)): Increased the figure size from (15, 15) to (20, 20) to make the image (and thus the text) appear larger in the notebook.

    Text Positioning:
        padding=10: Added padding to ensure the text doesn't overlap with the bounding box.
        Dynamic Text Positioning: Adjusts the y-coordinate of the text to place it above the bounding box if space allows; otherwise, it places it below.

3. Implementing the Revised Function

Replace your existing visualize_predictions function with the revised version above. Here's how you can integrate it into your workflow:
3.1. Complete Revised Workflow with Enhanced Visualization

# Step 1: Install Required Libraries (if not already installed)

# Step 2: Import Necessary Modules
import easyocr
from transformers import TrOCRProcessor, VisionEncoderDecoderModel
import torch
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw, ImageFont
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
import os

# Step 3: Initialize EasyOCR and TrOCR Models
# Initialize EasyOCR reader
reader = easyocr.Reader(['en'], gpu=torch.cuda.is_available())

# Load the finetuned TrOCR model and processor
model_path = "./trocr-finetuned"  # Ensure this path is correct
processor = TrOCRProcessor.from_pretrained(model_path)
model = VisionEncoderDecoderModel.from_pretrained(model_path)

# Move model to GPU if available
device = torch.device('cuda' if torch.cuda.is_available() else 'cpu')
model.to(device)

print(f"Model loaded to {device}")

# Step 4: Detect Words Using EasyOCR
# Define the path to your full-page image
image_path = "/content/my_journal_page_thresholded.png"

# Open the image using PIL
image = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")

# Perform word detection using EasyOCR
# results: list of tuples (bounding_box, text, confidence)
results = reader.readtext(image_path, detail=1, paragraph=False)

print(f"Detected {len(results)} words.")

# Step 5: Sort Bounding Boxes in Reading Order
def sort_bounding_boxes(results):
    """
    Sort bounding boxes from top to bottom and left to right.
    """
    # Extract bounding boxes and associated data
    data = []
    for result in results:
        bbox = result[0]  # Bounding box
        text = result[1]  # Detected text (unused)
        conf = result[2]  # Confidence (optional)
        # Calculate the average y-coordinate for top of the box
        avg_y = np.mean([point[1] for point in bbox])
        data.append((bbox, avg_y))
    
    # Sort primarily by avg_y (top to bottom), then by x-coordinate (left to right)
    sorted_data = sorted(data, key=lambda x: (x[1], x[0][0][0]))
    sorted_bboxes = [item[0] for item in sorted_data]
    return sorted_bboxes

sorted_bboxes = sort_bounding_boxes(results)

# Step 6: Extract Word Images and Recognize Text with TrOCR
def extract_word_image(image, bbox, padding=5):
    """
    Extracts a word image from the full-page image using the bounding box.
    Adds padding to include surrounding pixels.
    """
    # Unpack bounding box coordinates
    x1, y1 = bbox[0]
    x2, y2 = bbox[2]
    
    # Add padding
    x1 = max(int(x1) - padding, 0)
    y1 = max(int(y1) - padding, 0)
    x2 = min(int(x2) + padding, image.width)
    y2 = min(int(y2) + padding, image.height)
    
    # Crop the image
    cropped_image = image.crop((x1, y1, x2, y2))
    
    return cropped_image

def recognize_text(word_image, processor, model, device):
    """
    Recognizes text from a word image using TrOCR.
    """
    # Preprocess the image
    pixel_values = processor(images=word_image, return_tensors="pt").pixel_values
    pixel_values = pixel_values.to(device)
    
    # Generate token IDs
    with torch.no_grad():
        generated_ids = model.generate(pixel_values)
    
    # Decode the token IDs to get the recognized text
    generated_text = processor.batch_decode(generated_ids, skip_special_tokens=True)[0]
    
    return generated_text

# Initialize list to hold recognized texts
recognized_texts = []

# Iterate over sorted bounding boxes
for bbox in sorted_bboxes:
    # Extract word image
    word_img = extract_word_image(image, bbox, padding=5)
    
    # Recognize text using TrOCR
    text = recognize_text(word_img, processor, model, device)
    
    recognized_texts.append(text)

# Combine recognized texts into a single string
final_text = ' '.join(recognized_texts)

print("Recognized Text:\n")
print(final_text)

# Step 7: Assemble Recognized Text into a Document
# Save the final text to a .txt file
output_text_path = "/content/recognized_journal_page.txt"

with open(output_text_path, "w") as f:
    f.write(final_text)

print(f"Recognized text saved to {output_text_path}")

# Step 8: (Optional) Visualize Bounding Boxes and Recognized Text
def visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, font_size=20, padding=10):
    """
    Draws bounding boxes and recognized text on the image.

    Parameters:
    - image (PIL.Image): The original image.
    - sorted_bboxes (list): List of bounding boxes sorted in reading order.
    - recognized_texts (list): List of recognized texts corresponding to the bounding boxes.
    - font_size (int): Size of the font for the recognized text.
    - padding (int): Padding around the text background for better visibility.
    """
    from PIL import ImageDraw, ImageFont
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

    # Convert image to RGB if not already
    image = image.convert("RGB")
    draw = ImageDraw.Draw(image)

    # Specify a font. You can change the path to a .ttf file if desired.
    try:
        # Attempt to use a TrueType font
        font = ImageFont.truetype("arial.ttf", font_size)
    except IOError:
        # Fallback to the default PIL font if the specified font is not found
        print("Specified font not found. Using default font.")
        font = ImageFont.load_default()

    # Iterate over bounding boxes and recognized texts
    for bbox, text in zip(sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
        # Draw bounding box
        draw.line(
            [tuple(bbox[0]), tuple(bbox[1]), tuple(bbox[2]), tuple(bbox[3]), tuple(bbox[0])],
            fill="red",
            width=2
        )

        # Calculate position for text (top-left corner of the bounding box)
        x, y = bbox[0]

        # Calculate text size using ImageFont.getbbox (returns (left, top, right, bottom))
        text_bbox = font.getbbox(text)
        text_width = text_bbox[2] - text_bbox[0]
        text_height = text_bbox[3] - text_bbox[1]

        # Adjust y position to place text above the bounding box
        y_text = y - text_height - padding if y - text_height - padding > 0 else y + padding

        # Draw text background rectangle
        draw.rectangle([x, y_text, x + text_width, y_text + text_height], fill="yellow")

        # Draw text
        draw.text((x, y_text), text, fill="black", font=font)

    # Display the image with increased figure size for better visibility
    plt.figure(figsize=(20, 20))  # Increased from (15, 15) to (20, 20)
    plt.imshow(image)
    plt.axis('off')
    plt.show()

# Visualize the predictions with increased font size
visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, font_size=20, padding=10)

# Step 9: Save Recognized Text to a File (Already Done in Step 7)

4. Additional Enhancements for Better Visualization
4.1. Dynamic Font Sizing Based on Image Resolution

If you're working with images of varying resolutions, you might want to dynamically adjust the font size based on the image's dimensions.

def visualize_predictions_dynamic_font(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, padding=10):
    """
    Draws bounding boxes and recognized text on the image with dynamic font sizing.

    Parameters:
    - image (PIL.Image): The original image.
    - sorted_bboxes (list): List of bounding boxes sorted in reading order.
    - recognized_texts (list): List of recognized texts corresponding to the bounding boxes.
    - padding (int): Padding around the text background for better visibility.
    """
    from PIL import ImageDraw, ImageFont
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

    # Convert image to RGB if not already
    image = image.convert("RGB")
    draw = ImageDraw.Draw(image)

    # Calculate dynamic font size based on image height
    image_width, image_height = image.size
    font_size = max(15, image_height // 100)  # Adjust denominator for desired scaling

    # Specify a font. You can change the path to a .ttf file if desired.
    try:
        # Attempt to use a TrueType font
        font = ImageFont.truetype("arial.ttf", font_size)
    except IOError:
        # Fallback to the default PIL font if the specified font is not found
        print("Specified font not found. Using default font.")
        font = ImageFont.load_default()

    # Iterate over bounding boxes and recognized texts
    for bbox, text in zip(sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
        # Draw bounding box
        draw.line(
            [tuple(bbox[0]), tuple(bbox[1]), tuple(bbox[2]), tuple(bbox[3]), tuple(bbox[0])],
            fill="red",
            width=2
        )

        # Calculate position for text (top-left corner of the bounding box)
        x, y = bbox[0]

        # Calculate text size using ImageFont.getbbox
        text_bbox = font.getbbox(text)
        text_width = text_bbox[2] - text_bbox[0]
        text_height = text_bbox[3] - text_bbox[1]

        # Adjust y position to place text above the bounding box
        y_text = y - text_height - padding if y - text_height - padding > 0 else y + padding

        # Draw text background rectangle
        draw.rectangle([x, y_text, x + text_width, y_text + text_height], fill="yellow")

        # Draw text
        draw.text((x, y_text), text, fill="black", font=font)

    # Display the image with increased figure size for better visibility
    plt.figure(figsize=(20, 20))
    plt.imshow(image)
    plt.axis('off')
    plt.show()

# Use the dynamic font size visualization
visualize_predictions_dynamic_font(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, padding=10)

Explanation:

    Dynamic Font Size Calculation:
        font_size = max(15, image_height // 100): Adjusts the font size based on the image height to maintain consistency across different image resolutions. Modify the divisor (100 in this case) to scale the font size appropriately.

Apologies for the continued issues with the text size in the visualize_predictions function. To ensure that the recognized text appears larger and more readable, we'll make the following key revisions:

    Use a Custom TrueType Font: Instead of relying on fonts like "arial.ttf" which may not be available in your environment, we'll download a free and widely supported TrueType font (e.g., Roboto) directly within the function.

    Increase Font Size Parameter: We'll set a larger font_size to ensure the text overlays are prominent.

    Adjust Figure Size for Better Visibility: Increasing the figsize in Matplotlib will make the image (and thus the overlaid text) appear larger in your notebook.

Below is the revised visualize_predictions function incorporating these changes:

def visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, font_size=30, padding=10):
    """
    Draws bounding boxes and recognized text on the image with increased text size.

    Parameters:
    - image (PIL.Image): The original image.
    - sorted_bboxes (list): List of bounding boxes sorted in reading order.
    - recognized_texts (list): List of recognized texts corresponding to the bounding boxes.
    - font_size (int): Size of the font for the recognized text.
    - padding (int): Padding around the text background for better visibility.
    """
    from PIL import ImageDraw, ImageFont
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    import requests
    import os

    # Convert image to RGB if not already
    image = image.convert("RGB")
    draw = ImageDraw.Draw(image)

    # Define the font URL and path
    font_url = "https://github.com/google/fonts/raw/main/apache/roboto/Roboto-Regular.ttf"
    font_path = "/content/Roboto-Regular.ttf"

    # Download the font if it doesn't exist
    if not os.path.exists(font_path):
        response = requests.get(font_url)
        with open(font_path, 'wb') as f:
            f.write(response.content)

    # Load the downloaded font with the desired font size
    try:
        font = ImageFont.truetype(font_path, font_size)
    except IOError:
        print("Failed to load the specified font. Using default font.")
        font = ImageFont.load_default()

    # Iterate over bounding boxes and recognized texts
    for bbox, text in zip(sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
        # Draw bounding box
        draw.line(
            [tuple(bbox[0]), tuple(bbox[1]), tuple(bbox[2]), tuple(bbox[3]), tuple(bbox[0])],
            fill="red",
            width=2
        )

        # Calculate position for text (top-left corner of the bounding box)
        x, y = bbox[0]

        # Calculate text size using ImageFont.getbbox
        text_bbox = font.getbbox(text)
        text_width = text_bbox[2] - text_bbox[0]
        text_height = text_bbox[3] - text_bbox[1]

        # Adjust y position to place text above the bounding box
        y_text = y - text_height - padding if y - text_height - padding > 0 else y + padding

        # Draw text background rectangle
        draw.rectangle([x, y_text, x + text_width, y_text + text_height], fill="yellow")

        # Draw text
        draw.text((x, y_text), text, fill="black", font=font)

    # Display the image with increased figure size for better visibility
    plt.figure(figsize=(20, 20))  # Increased from (15, 15) to (20, 20)
    plt.imshow(image)
    plt.axis('off')
    plt.show()

Explanation of Revisions

    Downloading and Using a Custom Font:

        Why: Default fonts or commonly referenced fonts like "arial.ttf" may not be available in the Colab environment, leading to fallback behaviors that don't respect the desired font_size.

        How:
            Font Selection: Chose Roboto, a free and widely supported font from Google Fonts.
            Download Mechanism: The function checks if the font file (Roboto-Regular.ttf) exists in the /content/ directory. If not, it downloads the font directly from the GitHub repository hosting Google Fonts.
            Loading the Font: Utilizes ImageFont.truetype to load the downloaded font with the specified font_size.

    Increasing the font_size:

        Why: A larger font_size ensures that the overlaid recognized text is more prominent and readable.

        How:
            Parameter Adjustment: Set font_size=30 by default. You can further increase this value if needed.
            Function Parameter: Made font_size a parameter for flexibility, allowing you to adjust it when calling the function.

    Adjusting the Figure Size in Matplotlib:

        Why: The display size of the image in the notebook affects the perceived size of the overlaid text. A larger figure size makes both the image and the text appear bigger.

        How:
            Figure Size Parameter: Changed figsize from (15, 15) to (20, 20). You can adjust these values based on your specific needs and the resolution of your image.

    Handling Font Loading Failures:

        Why: If, for any reason, the custom font fails to load (e.g., network issues during download), the function gracefully falls back to the default PIL font to prevent the entire visualization from breaking.

        How:
            Try-Except Block: Attempts to load the custom font and catches any IOError, defaulting to ImageFont.load_default() if necessary.

Usage Example

After incorporating the revised visualize_predictions function, you can use it as follows:

# Assuming you have already detected words and recognized texts
# sorted_bboxes = [...]  # Your sorted bounding boxes
# recognized_texts = [...]  # Your recognized texts

# Call the revised visualization function
visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, font_size=30, padding=10)

Adjusting Parameters

    font_size: Increase this value to make the text even larger. For example, font_size=40.

visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, font_size=40, padding=10)

padding: Modify the padding to ensure the text background doesn't overlap with the bounding boxes or other image elements.

    visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, font_size=30, padding=15)

Final Notes

    Font Availability: The function automatically downloads the Roboto font if it's not present. Ensure that your runtime has internet access to facilitate this download.

    Figure Size: If the text still appears too small, consider further increasing the figsize parameter in plt.figure(figsize=(20, 20)). For example, figsize=(25, 25).

    Image Resolution: High-resolution images will render text more clearly when displayed with larger figure sizes and font sizes.

By implementing these revisions, the recognized text overlays should now appear significantly larger and more readable in your visualizations. If you encounter any further issues or need additional adjustments, feel free to ask!

Apologies for the inconvenience you're experiencing. Let's focus on revising the visualize_predictions function to ensure that the recognized text appears larger and more readable. We'll address the AttributeError by ensuring that the font is correctly downloaded and loaded, and we'll adjust the font size and figure dimensions accordingly.
Revised visualize_predictions Function

def visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, font_size=30, padding=10):
    """
    Draws bounding boxes and recognized text on the image with increased text size.

    Parameters:
    - image (PIL.Image): The original image.
    - sorted_bboxes (list): List of bounding boxes sorted in reading order.
    - recognized_texts (list): List of recognized texts corresponding to the bounding boxes.
    - font_size (int): Size of the font for the recognized text.
    - padding (int): Padding around the text background for better visibility.
    """
    from PIL import ImageDraw, ImageFont
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    import requests
    import os

    # Convert image to RGB if not already
    image = image.convert("RGB")
    draw = ImageDraw.Draw(image)

    # Define the font URL and local path
    font_url = "https://github.com/google/fonts/raw/main/apache/roboto/Roboto-Regular.ttf"
    font_path = "/content/Roboto-Regular.ttf"

    # Download the font if it doesn't exist
    if not os.path.exists(font_path):
        try:
            print("Downloading Roboto font...")
            response = requests.get(font_url)
            response.raise_for_status()  # Raise an error for bad status
            with open(font_path, 'wb') as f:
                f.write(response.content)
            print("Roboto font downloaded successfully.")
        except requests.exceptions.RequestException as e:
            print(f"Failed to download the font: {e}")
            print("Using default font.")
            font_path = None

    # Load the font
    if font_path and os.path.exists(font_path):
        try:
            font = ImageFont.truetype(font_path, font_size)
        except IOError:
            print("Failed to load the downloaded font. Using default font.")
            font = ImageFont.load_default()
    else:
        font = ImageFont.load_default()

    # Iterate over bounding boxes and recognized texts
    for bbox, text in zip(sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
        # Draw bounding box
        draw.line(
            [tuple(bbox[0]), tuple(bbox[1]), tuple(bbox[2]), tuple(bbox[3]), tuple(bbox[0])],
            fill="red",
            width=2
        )

        # Calculate position for text (top-left corner of the bounding box)
        x, y = bbox[0]

        # Calculate text size using ImageFont.getbbox
        if hasattr(font, "getbbox"):
            text_bbox = font.getbbox(text)
            text_width = text_bbox[2] - text_bbox[0]
            text_height = text_bbox[3] - text_bbox[1]
        else:
            # Fallback for older Pillow versions
            text_width, text_height = draw.textsize(text, font=font)

        # Adjust y position to place text above the bounding box
        y_text = y - text_height - padding if y - text_height - padding > 0 else y + padding

        # Draw text background rectangle
        draw.rectangle([x, y_text, x + text_width, y_text + text_height], fill="yellow")

        # Draw text
        draw.text((x, y_text), text, fill="black", font=font)

    # Display the image with increased figure size for better visibility
    plt.figure(figsize=(20, 20))  # Increased from (15, 15) to (20, 20)
    plt.imshow(image)
    plt.axis('off')
    plt.show()

Explanation of Revisions

    Downloading the Roboto Font:
        Purpose: Ensures that a larger and more readable TrueType font is available for overlaying recognized text.
        Implementation:
            Font URL: Uses the direct link to the Roboto-Regular.ttf file from Google's GitHub repository.
            Download Check: Checks if the font file already exists in /content/. If not, it attempts to download it.
            Error Handling: Catches any exceptions during the download process. If the download fails, it falls back to using the default PIL font.

    Loading the Font:
        Purpose: Loads the downloaded Roboto font with the specified font_size.
        Implementation:
            Success: If the font is downloaded and loaded successfully, it uses ImageFont.truetype with the desired font_size (defaulted to 30).
            Failure: If loading the custom font fails for any reason, it falls back to ImageFont.load_default(), which ensures that the function doesn't break.

    Adjusting Text Size and Position:
        Font Size Parameter (font_size=30): Sets the font size to 30 by default, which is larger than typical default sizes. You can adjust this value based on your preferences.
        Padding Parameter (padding=10): Adds a 10-pixel padding around the text background to enhance readability and prevent overlap with bounding boxes.
        Dynamic Text Positioning: Ensures that the text is placed above the bounding box if there's enough space; otherwise, it places the text below the box.

    Compatibility with Different Pillow Versions:
        ImageFont.getbbox: Used to calculate the bounding box of the text, which provides the width and height for the background rectangle.
        Fallback Mechanism: If the getbbox method isn't available (in older Pillow versions), it falls back to using ImageDraw.textsize to calculate text dimensions.

    Increasing the Figure Size:
        plt.figure(figsize=(20, 20)): Enlarges the displayed image in the notebook to make both the image and the overlaid text more prominent. You can further increase this size if needed (e.g., (25, 25)).

Usage Instructions

    Replace Your Existing Function:
        Replace your current visualize_predictions function with the revised version provided above.

    Ensure Internet Access:
        Since the function attempts to download the Roboto font, ensure that your Colab environment has internet access.

    Run the Function:
        After detecting words and recognizing texts, call the visualize_predictions function with the appropriate parameters.

    visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, font_size=30, padding=10)

    Adjust Parameters if Needed:
        Font Size: If the text is still not sufficiently large, consider increasing the font_size parameter.
        Figure Size: Modify the figsize in plt.figure for larger or smaller displays.
        Padding: Adjust the padding parameter to ensure optimal spacing between text and bounding boxes.

Example Call with Increased Font Size

# Call the revised visualization function with a larger font size
visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, font_size=40, padding=15)

Troubleshooting Tips

    Font Download Issues:
        If the function prints "Failed to download the font: ..." and uses the default font instead, ensure that:
            The font URL is accessible.
            Your Colab environment has internet access.
            There are no firewall or network restrictions blocking the download.

    Font Not Displaying Correctly:
        If the text still appears small or not as expected, try increasing the font_size further.
        Ensure that the figsize parameter in plt.figure is set to a sufficiently large value to accommodate the larger text.

    Alternative Fonts:

        If Roboto doesn't suit your needs or fails to load, you can replace the font_url with another TrueType font's direct download link.

        For example, to use Open Sans, change the font_url and font_path accordingly.

        font_url = "https://github.com/google/fonts/raw/main/apache/opensans/OpenSans-Regular.ttf"
        font_path = "/content/OpenSans-Regular.ttf"

Final Notes

By ensuring that the font is correctly downloaded and loaded, and by adjusting the font_size and figsize, the visualize_predictions function should now display the recognized text in a larger and more readable format. If you continue to encounter issues or need further assistance, please let me know!

Revised visualize_predictions Function

def visualize_predictions(image, sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts, font_size=30, padding=10):
    """
    Draws bounding boxes and recognized text on the image with increased text size.

    Parameters:
    - image (PIL.Image): The original image.
    - sorted_bboxes (list): List of bounding boxes sorted in reading order.
    - recognized_texts (list): List of recognized texts corresponding to the bounding boxes.
    - font_size (int): Size of the font for the recognized text.
    - padding (int): Padding around the text background for better visibility.
    """
    from PIL import ImageDraw, ImageFont
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    import os

    # Convert image to RGB if not already
    image = image.convert("RGB")
    draw = ImageDraw.Draw(image)

    # Specify a font path that's available in Google Colab
    font_path = "/usr/share/fonts/truetype/dejavu/DejaVuSans.ttf"

    # Check if the font file exists
    if os.path.exists(font_path):
        try:
            # Load the DejaVuSans font with the specified font size
            font = ImageFont.truetype(font_path, font_size)
        except IOError:
            print("Failed to load DejaVuSans font. Using default font.")
            font = ImageFont.load_default()
    else:
        print("DejaVuSans font not found. Using default font.")
        font = ImageFont.load_default()

    # Iterate over bounding boxes and recognized texts
    for bbox, text in zip(sorted_bboxes, recognized_texts):
        # Draw bounding box
        draw.line(
            [tuple(bbox[0]), tuple(bbox[1]), tuple(bbox[2]), tuple(bbox[3]), tuple(bbox[0])],
            fill="red",
            width=2
        )

        # Calculate position for text (top-left corner of the bounding box)
        x, y = bbox[0]

        # Calculate text size using ImageFont.getbbox or ImageFont.getsize for compatibility
        if hasattr(font, "getbbox"):
            text_bbox = font.getbbox(text)
            text_width = text_bbox[2] - text_bbox[0]
            text_height = text_bbox[3] - text_bbox[1]
        else:
            # Fallback for older Pillow versions
            text_width, text_height = draw.textsize(text, font=font)

        # Adjust y position to place text above the bounding box
        y_text = y - text_height - padding if y - text_height - padding > 0 else y + padding

        # Draw text background rectangle
        draw.rectangle([x, y_text, x + text_width, y_text + text_height], fill="yellow")

        # Draw text
        draw.text((x, y_text), text, fill="black", font=font)

    # Display the image with increased figure size for better visibility
    plt.figure(figsize=(20, 20))  # Increased from (15, 15) to (20, 20)
    plt.imshow(image)
    plt.axis('off')
    plt.show()

Explanation of Revisions

    Using a System-Installed Font (DejaVuSans.ttf):

        Reason: Downloading external fonts can lead to issues like corrupted files or unsupported formats. Google Colab typically has DejaVuSans.ttf pre-installed, ensuring reliability.

        Implementation:
            Font Path: Set to /usr/share/fonts/truetype/dejavu/DejaVuSans.ttf, which is commonly available in Colab environments.
            Existence Check: The function checks if the font file exists using os.path.exists(font_path).
            Loading the Font: Utilizes ImageFont.truetype to load the font with the desired font_size.
            Fallback Mechanism: If the font fails to load for any reason, the function gracefully falls back to the default PIL font using ImageFont.load_default(), preventing the entire function from breaking.

    Adjusting Font Size (font_size=30):

        Reason: A larger font size ensures that the overlaid recognized text is more prominent and readable.

        Implementation:
            Parameterization: font_size is set to 30 by default but can be adjusted when calling the function based on specific needs.
            Loading the Font: The specified font_size is applied when loading DejaVuSans.ttf.

    Increasing Figure Size (figsize=(20, 20)):

        Reason: Enlarging the display area makes both the image and the overlaid text appear larger within the notebook interface.

        Implementation:
            Matplotlib Figure Size: Changed from (15, 15) to (20, 20) to provide a more spacious display. You can further adjust these values (e.g., (25, 25)) if needed.

    Handling Text Size Calculation:
        Compatibility:
            Modern Pillow Versions: Use ImageFont.getbbox to calculate the bounding box of the text, providing precise width and height.
            Older Pillow Versions: Fall back to using ImageDraw.textsize to ensure compatibility.
        Implementation:
            Conditional Check: if hasattr(font, "getbbox"): determines if the getbbox method is available.
            Fallback: If getbbox isn't available, draw.textsize is used to calculate text_width and text_height.

    Dynamic Text Positioning:

        Reason: Ensures that the text label doesn't overlap with the bounding box by intelligently placing it either above or below based on available space.

        Implementation:
            Calculation: y_text is adjusted to position the text above the bounding box if there's sufficient space; otherwise, it places the text below.

    Enhanced Error Messages:

        Reason: Provides clear feedback if the font fails to load, aiding in troubleshooting.

Understanding OCR issues

Resetting and accessing

I’m thinking through how to reset the runtime in Colab to refresh terminal sessions. Start by navigating to 'Runtime' menu, then select either 'Restart runtime' or 'Factory reset runtime', and confirm the action.

Resetting and re-facing

I'm sorry to hear that you're experiencing issues with the terminal in Google Colab Enterprise. Yes, you can reset the runtime, which will effectively restart your entire Colab environment, including any terminal sessions. Here's how you can do it:
Steps to Reset the Runtime in Google Colab Enterprise

    Navigate to the Runtime Menu:

    At the top of your Colab notebook interface, locate and click on the Runtime menu.

    Select "Restart runtime":

    From the dropdown, choose Restart runtime.... This option will restart your Colab environment, terminating all current processes, including any unresponsive terminal sessions.

    Confirm the Restart:

    A dialog box will appear asking you to confirm the restart. Click Yes to proceed.

    Wait for the Runtime to Restart:

    Colab will display a message indicating that the runtime is restarting. This may take a few moments.

    Reopen the Terminal (If Needed):

    After the runtime has restarted, you can reopen the terminal:
        Accessing Terminal:
            If you had previously opened a terminal session, you might need to reopen it. Depending on your Colab Enterprise setup, this could be done via a specific menu option or toolbar button. Look for options labeled Terminal, Shell, or similar.
        Launching a New Terminal:
            If the terminal doesn't reopen automatically, try launching a new one through the provided interface in your Colab Enterprise environment.

Additional Tips

    Saving Your Work:

    Before resetting the runtime, ensure that you've saved all necessary work. While Colab automatically saves your notebook, any variables, temporary files, or unsaved data in the terminal will be lost.

    Interrupting the Runtime:

    If the terminal is unresponsive but you prefer not to restart the entire runtime, you can try interrupting it:

        Go to Runtime > Interrupt execution.

        This might stop the unresponsive process without a full restart.

    Factory Reset Runtime:

    If restarting the runtime doesn't resolve the issue, you can perform a factory reset:

        Navigate to Runtime > Factory reset runtime....

        Confirm the action when prompted.

        Note: Factory resetting will remove all installed packages and uploaded files. You'll need to reinstall any dependencies and re-upload necessary files after the reset.

    Check Internet Connection:

    Sometimes, unresponsiveness can be due to connectivity issues. Ensure that your internet connection is stable.

    Browser Refresh:

    If the terminal remains unresponsive after a runtime reset, try refreshing your browser page or reopening the notebook.

Contacting Support

If you've tried the above steps and the terminal is still not responding, consider reaching out to your organization's IT support or the Google Colab support team for further assistance. Provide them with details of the issue and the steps you've already taken to troubleshoot.

I hope this helps you resolve the terminal issue in your Colab Enterprise environment. If you have any more questions or need further assistance, feel free to ask!
